Expression statements add one generic stack discard to the invariant source
compiler vocabulary. The focused runtime is 2,691 / 1,329 versus the 1,984 /
961 core, and its compiler is 6,175 / 2,607 versus 5,471 / 2,378. The runtime
dependency graph enables only the arena's `stack-operations` module; it includes
no exceptions, functions, objects, or locals. Reduced compilers leave the first
ordinary expression statement unconsumed, while existing specialized
assignment statements continue to compile without this gate.

Implicit completions are compiler-only and therefore have no runtime row. The
focused compiler is 5,772 / 2,441 versus the 5,471 / 2,378 minimal compiler,
an increment of 301 raw / 63 gzip bytes. A separate `#[cfg]` implementation
keeps the omitted completion function structurally identical to its earlier
form: the independently measured expression-statements compiler remains
exactly 6,175 / 2,607 after this addition.

Nonterminal conditionals are also compiler-only. Their 8,207 / 3,100 artifact
adds 747 raw / 135 gzip bytes to the 7,460 / 2,965 control-flow compiler. The
focused feature graph adds no bytecode capability beyond `control-flow`, while
the omitted build retains the terminal conditional implementation.

Nested conditionals extend that compiler to 8,588 / 3,494, an increment of
381 raw / 394 gzip bytes over nonterminal conditionals. The larger gzip delta
reflects the explicit frame-state loop rather than another repeated recursive
parser path. A 2,048-level source and runtime probe completes without native
parser or VM call frames. Omitting the feature preserves the nonterminal
artifact exactly at 8,207 / 3,100 and adds no runtime capability.

Nested loops generalize the conditional worklist rather than wrapping the old
parser in another layer. The focused compiler is 8,410 / 3,546: consolidating
the two frame paths makes it 178 raw bytes smaller and 52 gzip bytes larger
than the conditional-only implementation. A mixed loop/conditional program and
a 2,048-level loop source both compile and execute, including relocated targets
inside functions. `nested-loops` adds only `control-flow` to the runtime graph.
Omitting it preserves the nested-conditional artifact exactly at 8,588 / 3,494.

Nonterminal exceptions compose handler cleanup with control-flow joins. Their
focused compiler is 9,713 / 3,503 versus 7,121 / 3,030 for terminal exceptions,
an increment of 2,592 raw / 473 gzip bytes. Normal try completion removes its
handler and jumps over the catch; catch fallthrough discards its pending reason.
Both targets relocate inside function bodies. Omitting the feature preserves
the terminal exception artifact exactly at 7,121 / 3,030.

Nested exceptions extend that join-aware parser with explicit worklist frames.
Their focused compiler is 11,449 / 4,515 and their runtime graph remains exactly
`control-flow` plus `exceptions`; the VM gains no nesting feature or depth
counter. Live tests cover nested fallthrough, returns across multiple handlers,
rethrows to an outer handler, nesting from a catch, and 2,048 active guest
handler records. Omitting nesting preserves the nonterminal exception compiler
at 9,713 / 3,503.

Local assignment expressions compose the 4,012 / 1,798 locals runtime with
the removable stack operation for a 4,786 / 2,066 live artifact. Their compiler
is 8,823 / 3,652, an increment of 477 raw / 133 gzip bytes over locals alone.
Omitting the feature retains the former locals artifacts exactly and leaves
plain assignment unconsumed in expression position.

Captured assignment expressions compose the 6,285 / 2,728 environment-cell
runtime with a result-preserving store for a 7,186 / 3,047 live artifact. Their
compiler is 18,773 / 7,524 versus 17,884 / 7,449 for captured environments.
Omitting the feature removes its crate and opcode; it does not acquire the
stack-operations crate used by uncaptured assignment.

Property assignment expressions compose the 14,473 / 5,775 object runtime
with a result-preserving object store for a 15,713 / 6,242 live artifact. Their
compiler is 11,619 / 4,787. The compiler recognizes local, captured, receiver,
and nested dot targets, while the focused runtime links neither environments
nor stack operations. Omitting the feature removes its crate and opcode and
leaves ordinary object reads and literal stores unchanged.

Array indexing is compiler-only and raises the 6,224 / 2,677 literal compiler
to 6,460 / 2,814 while reusing the existing array read instruction. Result-
preserving indexed assignment composes the 8,768 / 3,532 array runtime into a
10,034 / 4,006 artifact; its compiler is 11,176 / 4,590. Omission removes the
dedicated crate and opcode and does not link objects, environments, locals, or
stack operations into the focused runtime.

Conditional expressions add 554 raw / 224 gzip bytes to the compiler over the
7,460 / 2,965 control-flow baseline, producing an 8,014 / 3,194 focused
artifact. The runtime is byte-for-byte the same control-flow machine: the
syntax reuses boolean conversion and existing conditional and unconditional
jumps. Omission leaves the token unconsumed and removes the parser and
backpatching branch.

The `literal-expressions` rows compose the ordinary expression parser with
literal storage without changing either runtime. Numeric-only array and object
compilers remain 6,224 / 2,677 and 7,779 / 3,363 respectively. Enclosing local
values cost 8,786 / 3,705 for arrays or 10,244 / 4,301 for objects; selecting
both shares enough parser code to total 10,611 / 4,419 rather than their sum.

Strict string concatenation adds 175 raw bytes and 77 gzip bytes over literal
strings. It is its own opcode and capability crate, and does not link numeric
promotion or general JavaScript coercion.

The 6,072-byte Number-negation compiler can target boxed results without the
decimal/exponent literal parser. Before that parser boundary was separated,
the same probe was 24,258 bytes (17,770 gzip). This is a compiler-only removal;
the runtime Number-negation composition still includes the representation it
needs to preserve negative zero.

The minimal row links neither lexical binding nor function/capture parsing.
The locals row adds stable slots without closures. Basic functions use
zero-argument `CallValue` and ordinary local frames; parameter parsing,
evaluated argument lists, `CallValueArguments`, and `InitFrame` are a separate
compiler capability. Neither build acquires captured environments or a
recursive constructor. Captures add environment-cell analysis; named function
expressions add only their matching synchronous or asynchronous recursive
constructor. The default row additionally retains all currently supported
structural and scalar syntax. Re-run it with:

```sh
npm run measure:compiler
```

The lexical binding implementation now lives in one feature-gated
`microesnext-bindings` crate instead of five packages with the same arena and
frame representation. Exact pre/post builds kept environment cells at 6,285 /
2,728 bytes, closures with shared environments at 11,173 / 4,434, recursive
functions at 10,414 / 4,048, and functions with parameters at 10,897 / 4,357.
Receiver-aware calls decreased from 13,156 / 5,109 to 13,122 / 5,103. The
feature graph checker separately verifies that `locals`, `parameters`,
`receivers`, `environments`, and `recursive-functions` remain removable; the
package consolidation therefore adds neither code nor runtime dispatch.

Scalar representations are likewise modules of one feature-gated
`microesnext-values` package. Consolidating strings and atoms, symbols,
numbers, decimal conversion, and guest Error values leaves the focused live
artifacts unchanged: errors are 2,520 / 1,195 bytes, numbers 5,311 / 2,422,
strings 4,903 / 2,270, symbols 4,995 / 2,254, and string Number coercion
14,599 / 6,608. The compiler selects only `decimal-to-f64`, whose feature does
not enable the optional arena dependency. Package-level composition therefore
does not turn build-time numeric parsing into a runtime value dependency.

Arrays, iterator cursors, and Map/Set storage are feature-gated modules of
`microesnext-objects`. Their shared arena/table domain no longer requires four
published packages. Raw focused Wasm remains exactly 8,768 bytes for arrays,
3,599 for iterators, 3,285 for collections, 10,756 for array iteration, and
13,542 for collection entry iteration. Weak collections remain a 2,051-byte
composition. The object-package feature audit checks these modules separately,
so package unification does not make one representation imply another.


<!-- dev/microquickjs-experiment/docs/bytecode.md -->

# Bytecode study

MicroESNext inherits MicroQuickJS's compact stack bytecode. Instructions have a
one-byte opcode followed by zero, one, two, or four bytes of immediate data.
`mquickjs_opcode.h` is the authoritative instruction list: it declares each
instruction's encoded size, stack inputs and outputs, and operand format. The
same table drives interpretation, stack-depth analysis, relocation, dumping,
and 64-to-32-bit bytecode conversion.

Function bytecode carries a constant pool, argument and local-variable names,
captured-variable descriptors, a maximum operand-stack depth, source mapping,
and nested function bytecode. `fclosure` instantiates a nested function and
connects its captured variables to the enclosing frame. Calls, returns,
exceptions, and `finally` already have direct instructions.

## Arrow functions

Arrow functions do not require a new instruction. Their parameters and body
use ordinary function bytecode, and `fclosure` creates the function. A function
metadata bit distinguishes arrows so closure creation captures the enclosing
`this`, calls restore that captured value, and construction can be rejected.
Expression bodies compile as an expression followed by `return`.

## Named function expressions

A named expression has a lexical self-binding that is not visible in its
enclosing scope. The optional `recursive_closure` instruction composes the
ordinary closure and environment-cell capabilities: it creates one cell,
places it first in the capture list, constructs the closure, and initializes
the cell with that closure. Unnamed expressions, arrows, and declarations do
not pay for this constructor, and builds omitting `recursive-functions` reject
its opcode.

## Class constructors

`to_class_constructor` wraps a compact closure in a distinct property-bearing
record and creates its `.prototype.constructor` cycle. The ordinary call path
does not unwrap this record; the independently gated construction path does.
Constructor-only class declarations otherwise reuse receiver frames, argument
calls, property stores, and return selection rather than adding a parallel
execution model.

The compiler emits `construct_value_arguments` and
`select_constructed_result` before compiling any later property or call
suffixes. Constructor-member lookup uses ordinary `get_property`, because
`new namespace.Type()` constructs the resolved value rather than calling it as
a method of `namespace`. The selected instance or explicit object result then
enters the unchanged postfix path, allowing forms such as `new Type().read()`.

An omitted base constructor needs no bytecode extension. When enabled, the
compiler synthesizes an ordinary zero-parameter receiver frame ending in
`load_this` and `return_receiver_locals`, then applies `to_class_constructor`.
This keeps implicit construction entirely out of runtimes that only consume
explicitly authored bytecode.

The separate method composition uses `to_method`, `get_method`, and
`call_method_arguments`. `get_method` preserves the base immediately below the
resolved value; evaluated arguments follow it. The call instruction removes the
method and base, appends the base as the hidden receiver only for a callable
object, and enters the otherwise ordinary argument frame. Omitting method calls
leaves `get_property` and ordinary call bytecode unchanged.

Static methods require no fourth method opcode. The compiler selects the class
record rather than its `prototype`, applies `to_method`, and stores the result;
subsequent member calls use the same `get_method` and `call_method_arguments`
sequence as instance methods.

Anonymous class expressions use the independently gated `duplicate_top`
instruction while installing methods. The original class record stays beneath
the duplicate, decoration consumes the duplicate, and the original becomes the
expression result. A class with no methods needs no duplication. This avoids a
persistent temporary local and leaves declaration-only class bytecode and its
runtime unchanged.

Uncaptured local assignment expressions reuse that same `duplicate_top`
instruction. The right-hand value is duplicated, `store_local` consumes one
copy, and the other remains as the expression result. Statement assignment
continues to emit only `store_local`; builds omitting assignment expressions do
not decode stack duplication merely because they support mutable locals.

Captured assignment uses `store_environment_cell_result` instead. Its input is
`[cell, value]` and its output is `[value]`; the operation updates the traced
cell between those states. This is deliberately distinct from
`store_environment_cell`, which consumes both inputs for statement assignment,
and from `duplicate_top`, which cannot preserve a value around a cell without
extra stack movement.

Property assignment expressions use `set_property_result`. Its input is
`[object, key, value]` and its output is `[value]`; the object store occurs
between those states. Intermediate dot segments use ordinary `get_property`,
so a nested target adds no specialized traversal instruction. The opcode is
separate from `set_property`, which returns the object for literal assembly,
and `set_property_statement`, which leaves no result.

Computed array reads use the existing `get_index` instruction already required
by array storage. The compiler-only `array-indexing` gate therefore changes no
runtime. Array assignment expressions use `set_index_result`; its input is
`[array, index, value]` and its output is `[value]`. This is distinct from
`set_index`, whose object result lets literal construction append another
element. Intermediate mixed suffixes continue to use ordinary `get_property`
or `get_index` instructions.

## Receivers

Receiver-aware functions use `init_receiver_frame`, `load_this`, and
`return_receiver_locals`. The caller appends one hidden receiver after the
evaluated arguments. Frame initialization leaves the ordinary count and local
slots in their existing positions and keeps the receiver immediately after the
fixed local region. Temporary operands can therefore grow above it without
changing its address.

These instructions are optional rather than a new field in the invariant VM.
Ordinary closures and bytecode remain unchanged. The optional thenable driver
uses these instructions to invoke a guest `then` function with the source
object as its receiver; it schedules chained thenables as separate jobs.

## Strict equality

The optional `strict_equal` and `strict_not_equal` instructions each consume
two values and produce a tagged boolean. Their implementation is statically
composed with the enabled value representations: the identity-only build does
not link number or string storage, while scalar builds compare numbers and
string contents according to JavaScript strict-equality semantics. Collection
SameValueZero remains a distinct operation because its treatment of `NaN`
differs.

## Short-circuit logical operations

`jump_if_falsy_or_pop` implements `&&`; `jump_if_truthy_or_pop` implements
`||`. Each reads the top operand without copying it. When the operand selects
the expression result, the instruction jumps and leaves it in place. Otherwise
it pops that operand and falls through to the right expression. Forward targets
are ordinary relocation entries, so the same representation works inside
nested function bodies without native callbacks or an AST at runtime.

These instructions belong to `logical-operations`, not basic `control-flow`.
Consequently a machine needing loops and `if` but no logical expressions does
not decode them or link their truthiness composition.

Conditional source expressions need no additional instruction. The compiler
emits `to_boolean`, `jump_if_false_boolean`, the consequent, an unconditional
`jump`, and the alternate. Both targets use ordinary relocation records when
the expression is inside an appended function body. Thus a runtime capable of
`if` already has the complete execution vocabulary, while a reduced compiler
can omit the `?:` parser branch independently.

## Numeric targets

`const_f64` belongs to number representation; `add_number`,
`subtract_number`, `multiply_number`, and promoted relational operations
belong to the separate number-operations capability. Numeric equality and
storage can therefore omit all arithmetic decoder arms.

The compiler target is explicit. A JavaScript-number target emits an invariant
i30 opcode only when its result is statically known to fit, and otherwise emits
the promoted operation. A bounded-i30 target emits only the smaller integer
instruction family and reports overflow at execution. This is a build target,
not a language-visible claim that JavaScript integers stop at 30 bits.

Collection-literal expression support is entirely a compiler composition.
Both the reduced numeric loop and the general-expression loop emit the same
`new_array`/`set_index` and `new_object`/`set_property` instructions. Therefore
enabling richer literal source syntax adds no decoder branch or runtime
dispatch, and omitting it can discard parser code without changing bytecode
semantics.

## Untagged template literals

Bare template literals do not require a new instruction. Cooked literal pieces
enter the existing constant pool. Each substitution uses the ordinary
expression compiler, and the pieces are combined with the existing `add`
instruction, whose string coercion supplies template interpolation semantics.
Tagged templates are intentionally not part of this milestone.

The Rust engine currently keeps `ConcatenateStrings` removable and strict: it
accepts only two existing strings. This gives statically proven concatenation
a small implementation without pretending to provide the general coercion
required by template substitutions.

## Async functions and await

An async function body reuses ordinary frame, local, capture, and return
instructions. The optional `async_closure` constructor distinguishes its call
boundary from an ordinary closure: a call returns a pending Promise to the
caller while its body runs in an independent guest context. Builds omitting
`async-functions` neither decode that constructor nor link its closure and
invocation storage.

Declarations, anonymous function expressions, and arrows emit that same
constructor. A named recursive async expression emits
`recursive_async_closure`, whose separate composition crate adds a private
self cell to async closure storage. Builds needing async functions but not
recursive self identity do not decode or link that constructor.

`await` suspends after evaluating one operand. It retains the function's
arguments, locals, operand stack, program counter, exception state, and live
captured-variable references, then resumes with either a fulfilled value or a
thrown rejection.

The runtime stores suspended contexts in GC-visible engine objects and
schedules their continuation through the same bounded FIFO Promise job queue
used by `then`. A caller-supplied async frame array tracks only currently
running nested bodies; once a body reaches `await`, its heap continuation and
Promise reaction replace that scheduler frame. This preserves ordinary
bytecode on either side of an await while making suspension explicit.

The saved context contains the active stack range, frame and handler depths,
and program counter. The scheduler frame separately roots the caller context
and outer Promise. Captured mutable variables remain traced environment cells,
so compaction can relocate the entire graph without exposing raw offsets.

MicroESNext executes a resumed function on its ordinary compact VM stack. It
does not leave every async invocation permanently resident there: while the
function is suspended, its snapshot is an ordinary GC-visible value graph.
Fulfillment restores the awaited operand and continues after `await`;
rejection restores the same frame and enters its existing exception machinery
as though the await expression had thrown.

`try` records a catch target together with the current operand, call-frame, and
outer-handler depths. `end_try` removes that record on normal completion while
preserving the expression result. `throw`, including the abrupt completion
created by a rejected `await`, truncates the guest stack to the nearest record
and resumes at its catch target. None of these operations uses native Rust or
WebAssembly stack unwinding.

The bytecode format version is `2`. Version `1` readers cannot safely infer the
new function metadata or opcode numbering and must reject these artifacts.

Whenever the instruction table changes, tests must cover source execution,
bytecode compilation/loading, stack-size analysis, dumping, relocation, and
the 64-to-32-bit conversion path. An opcode working only in the interpreter is
not considered implemented.


<!-- dev/microquickjs-experiment/docs/rust-engine.md -->

# Rust engine invariants

MicroESNext is not an ordinary Rust object graph wrapped around a bytecode
loop. Its guest machine is a relocatable address space with explicit bounds.
The following rules preserve the properties for which MicroQuickJS is useful.

- Every guest `Value` and heap reference is exactly four bytes on Wasm32 and
  native targets.
- Runtime crates are `no_std`, receive their memory from the caller, and do not
  require libc or a global allocator. Compiler tooling may use `alloc` in a
  separate crate.
- Capacity is not permanently fixed. A caller can move a machine into a larger
  word slice. Heap offsets remain unchanged and the stack is copied against the
  new upper boundary. A Wasm controller can grow linear memory and rebind the
  same offset-based machine state.
- Durable relationships use word offsets. A Rust reference into the arena may
  not survive a call that can allocate or collect.
- The guest heap grows upward and its operand/frame stack grows downward in one
  fixed-capacity buffer.
- Guest calls, parsing, tracing, and suspension must not recurse on the native
  stack.
- Guest allocations do not become individual `Box`, `Vec`, `String`, `Rc`, or
  host allocator entries.
- Collection traces and compacts the arena. Debug tests must relocate live
  graphs frequently enough to expose stale offsets.
- Bytecode and immutable built-ins may be shared outside the mutable arena.
- Capabilities are independent Cargo features within a small set of coherent
  crates linked statically. The base opcode loop does not use trait objects,
  dynamic registries, or callbacks for omitted features.
- Capability bytecodes are feature-gated at decoding as well as execution.
  Bytes assigned to an omitted capability are invalid rather than accepted by
  a larger universal enum and rejected later by the VM wrapper.
- The execution crate's `core` module reads raw bytes and depends only on the
  invariant `microesnext-bytecode::core` vocabulary. It returns any non-core
  byte to the feature-gated typed decoder in `microesnext-vm`; the raw core is
  deliberately not an independent validator for extension opcodes.
- An awaited computation is a heap-resident VM frame. Rust `async fn` and its
  compiler-generated future are host integration options, not its guest
  representation.
- The optional execution `suspension` feature owns conversion between a live
  VM stack and a rooted continuation. The arena supplies generic stack-snapshot
  storage, but the execution `core` module contains no suspend, resume, or restore
  algorithm. Builds without `async` do not expose those machine operations.
- An async JavaScript call cannot suspend its ordinary caller at the first
  `await`. The optional execution `async-functions` layer therefore packages
  the closure and evaluated arguments, snapshots the caller, and starts the
  body in an independent guest context over the same arena. The scheduler can
  restore the caller with the outer promise while the body continuation enters
  that promise's reaction path. A bounded caller-owned frame array permits
  nested async entry without a native recursive call or hidden allocation.
- Guest exception handlers are explicit records in the guest stack. The
  optional execution `exceptions` module owns the handler-chain pointer and
  stack operations; `microesnext-vm` composes it only behind the `exceptions`
  feature, and the invariant opcode core has no exception state. A throw
  restores the recorded operand and call-frame depths without unwinding the
  native Rust stack. Suspension snapshots the active handler depth, so a
  rejected `await` follows exactly the same handler chain as a synchronous
  throw.
- Wasm exception references are an optional foreign-call optimization, not the
  representation of a JavaScript exception. A baseline artifact must remain
  usable by engines that cannot validate `exnref`; a separately selected build
  may catch and rethrow opaque host or foreign-Wasm exceptions unchanged.
- Scheduler and host references use O(1), generation-checked root slots. Raw
  moving heap offsets are not exposed as durable asynchronous handles.
- Native Promise adoption is an optional composition over compact Promise
  storage. It registers an identity reaction in the caller-owned queue rather
  than recursively following a chain on the Rust or WebAssembly stack. Its
  compact error dependency turns self-resolution into a guest TypeError
  rejection rather than an engine-control error.
- Receiver-aware invocation is an optional frame composition. It reuses the
  existing argument entry path with one hidden value after fixed locals; the
  invariant `Core`, ordinary frames, and builds omitting `receivers` are
  unchanged. Object thenable assimilation uses this boundary rather than a
  host callback or proxy.
- Promise resolving functions are compact callable records rather than guest
  closures. A resolve/reject pair shares one traced `alreadyResolved` cell and
  yields a payload-free VM outcome; optional machine state carries the call to
  its controller without changing the invariant core or the public outcome
  layout under Cargo feature unification.
- Thenable resolution is split into synchronous inspection and rooted jobs.
  Guest `then` code is never invoked inline while resolving a value. If its
  resolving function receives another thenable, execution returns a new job to
  the controller, preserving ECMAScript deferral without native recursion.
- Async completion is generic over a statically dispatched resolution policy.
  Its ordinary public entry points monomorphize with native Promise resolution;
  The scheduler `async-thenables` feature supplies a separate policy holding a
  bounded,
  caller-owned thenable-job queue. The same policy can park an `await` on an
  intermediate Promise before resolving an object operand, while native
  Promises and primitives retain the direct path. This adds no trait object,
  callback, or branch to a build that omits the composition.
- Packed byte strings, atom tables, objects, and closures remain arena blocks;
  they do not become Rust allocations merely because their implementations are
  split across crates.
- Small integers remain immediate i30 values in the invariant core. Optional
  IEEE-754 numbers use opaque two-word payloads from the values `numbers` feature, so
  arbitrary floating-point bits are never traced as guest references. Builds
  without `numbers` omit the arena block kind, typed opcodes, and implementation.
- Unary integer and JavaScript Number negation are separate capability modules
  from binary arithmetic. The promoted path preserves negative zero, while a
  build needing only multiplication or comparison retains neither negation
  implementation nor either negation opcode.
- Division and remainder are separate from each other and from ordinary
  arithmetic. This is not only taxonomy: linked Wasm showed that floating `%`
  retains a substantially larger helper than `/`. A division-only machine has
  no remainder opcode, implementation, or dispatch branch.
- Primitive Number coercion is a further capability boundary. Its typed result
  lets promoted negation consume a coercion result once, while its pre-existing
  i30 branch remains ahead of coercion. Unary plus materializes a boxed value
  only for NaN. String parsing and object coercion are deliberately absent
  rather than host-defined.
- String Number coercion is layered above that primitive module. It borrows the
  packed arena payload directly, validates the whole grammar, and allocates
  only the resulting boxed number when an immediate i30 cannot represent it.
  Power-of-two integer parsing keeps explicit guard/sticky bits; no temporary
  guest string participates in those paths.
- Decimal conversion is a separate leaf module shared by compiler literals and
  string coercion. Its exact fast path and bounded correctly-rounded fallback
  contain no allocator or runtime policy. Static Cargo composition removes the
  code and its call sites when neither consumer is selected.
- `typeof` is another leaf policy rather than a method on the VM. Its base
  build recognizes immediates, strings, ordinary objects, and closures;
  optional arena kinds extend the same direct match through Cargo feature
  unification. It allocates an ordinary string and requires no atom table.
- Ordinary strings, atom interning, and objects form three boundaries. A string
  value is not automatically retained or interned. Atoms add a rooted table;
  objects compose atoms for source-level property names. A string-only machine
  links neither atom nor property-table operations.
- Symbols are individually allocated identities whose optional descriptions do
  not participate in equality. Object and collection tables already compare
  compact `Value` identities, so symbols compose as keys without either table
  depending on the values `symbols` module. The arena symbol kind is feature-gated.
- Iterator positions are guest-arena state rather than native Rust cursors.
  The source-agnostic objects `iterators` module retains the iterable and
  current index across collection. Array and Map/Set traversal live in separate
  adapter modules, preserving both dependency and code-size isolation.
- SameValueZero is capability-aware without runtime registration. A standalone
  collection build compares compact identities directly. Cargo weak-feature
  forwarding statically adds string-content and boxed-number equality only
  when collections are composed with those scalar features.

The first Rust vertical slice deliberately supports only i30 constants,
addition, `await`, and return. It proves that the interpreter can suspend after
its Rust call stack has unwound, retain its operand stack through a compact
handle, and resume without a native continuation. Language coverage comes
after this representation is measured and hardened.


<!-- dev/microquickjs-experiment/docs/supported-es6.md -->

# Supported modern JavaScript subset

MicroESNext implements a focused set of newer primitives and syntax. This is
not a general claim of ECMAScript conformance.

## Syntax and async execution

`undefined`, `null`, `false`, and `true` compile to tagged values in the
invariant VM. They require no heap allocation and no optional capability.

Decimal and exponent literals compile to the optional `numbers` capability.
It preserves IEEE-754 values, including negative zero and NaN, while keeping
small integers as immediate i30 words. Addition of statically small integer
terms stays on the invariant opcode path; expressions requiring promotion use
the removable numeric path.

The i30 range is an internal immediate-value optimization, not a visible
JavaScript integer limit. With `numbers` enabled, the IEEE-754 precision
boundary is `2^53`: that value itself is representable, but `2^53 + 1` is not
distinct from it. Consequently `Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER` is `2^53 - 1`.
Integer-only compositions intentionally expose a smaller bounded subset;
full numeric compositions promote results that no longer fit an i30.

Compiler callers select that distinction through `CompileOptions`: the
default JavaScript target promotes, while `NumberMode::BoundedI30` produces
bytecode for a runtime that intentionally omits number storage and promoted
operations. The compiler retains direct i30 instructions for statically proven
small results in either target.

The bytecode generator is progressive as well as the runtime. Its current
Cargo gates independently remove number representation, promoted and bounded
number operators, strict equality, and logical operators. When a gate is
absent the parser does not consume that syntax, so callers receive the same
ordinary grammar error they would get for another unexpected token. The
`source-positions` feature controls whether `CompileError` retains the source
offset; compact build-time uses can omit that field. Array literals are an
independent parser feature. Object literals and property access compose the
object, atom, and string bytecode vocabulary, without adding those paths to an
array-only or scalar-only generator.

Conditional expressions are a compiler-only composition over control flow.
They evaluate the condition once, apply the runtime's selected truthiness
policy, execute exactly one branch, and associate from the right. Their branch
targets use the same relocation path inside nested functions as loops and
logical operators. Omitting `conditional-expressions` leaves `?` unconsumed and
adds no opcode or runtime feature.

The separate `nonterminal-conditionals` compiler gate lets either `if` branch
fall through to later statements. A branch ending in `return` or `throw` is
recognized as abrupt and receives no redundant join jump. Both alternate and
join targets use function relocation when needed. The further
`nested-conditionals` gate tracks consequent/alternate state, abruptness, and
patch positions in an explicit allocation-using worklist. It accepts nested
`if`/`else` without Rust recursion; a test compiles and executes 2,048 levels.
The further `nested-loops` gate adds loop start and exit frames to that same
worklist. Loops and conditionals may be interleaved to arbitrary source depth;
back edges, exits, joins, and function-body relocations remain direct bytecode
patches. Its own 2,048-level compile-and-run test consumes no Rust parser
frames. Exception regions use the independently removable worklist below.

Exception regions now have their own independently removable
`nested-exceptions` worklist. It tracks whether each source region is compiling
its `try` or `catch` side, patches catch targets directly, and emits one
`end_try` for every live handler crossed by a return. Throws continue through
the runtime's existing nearest-handler chain. The prerequisite
`nonterminal-exceptions` feature gives normally completed try and catch sides a
direct join over the catch body, releasing the handler record or caught value
before later statements. A test compiles and executes 2,048 nested regions
without Rust parser frames; the VM still has no source nesting counter or fixed
depth.

Expression statements are independently selectable. Any expression accepted
by the chosen compiler composition may be followed by `;` before a terminal
completion, including calls and result-preserving property or array
assignments. The compiler emits one generic `DropTop` operation, so adding a
new expression family does not require another statement opcode. This works at
top level and in function, method, constructor, loop, conditional, `try`, and
`catch` bodies. Omitting `expression-statements` removes its parser loop and
the stack-operation dispatch; specialized local and property assignment
statements retain their smaller direct paths.

The compiler-only `implicit-completions` feature accepts explicit `return;`
and bodies that reach their closing brace or the end of a script. Both produce
the invariant `undefined` value through existing return bytecode. Fall-through
from a terminal `try` closes its handler before returning; a fall-through catch
discards the pending exception first. Constructors retain their existing
receiver-return rule. Omitting the feature restores the original parser
implementation, requires a value-bearing `return` or `throw`, and changes no
runtime dependency or dispatch path.

Property-assignment statements are separately gated from object literals and
reads. The current source form accepts `local.name = value;` and constructor
receiver initialization such as `this.name = value;`. Its opcode consumes the
object, interned key, and value without leaving an unused expression result, so
it needs no general stack-cleanup feature. The independent
`local-assignment-expressions` composition adds plain `=` for uncaptured lexical
locals. It is right-associative, so `left = right = value` stores and evaluates
in JavaScript order, and the expression returns the stored value. The separate
`captured-assignment-expressions` composition extends the same grammar to an
outer capture or a locally held environment cell. It updates the shared value
seen by sibling closures and still returns the assignment result. Property
targets are another independent composition. It accepts local, captured, and
receiver bases with one or more dot-separated names, so `object.value = rhs`,
`outer.inner.value = rhs`, and `this.value = rhs` all return and store `rhs`.
Computed object keys, arbitrary expression bases, and compound assignment
remain separate compositions because they need different storage and stack
mechanics.

Array indexing is independently selectable from array literals. It accepts an
ordinary supported expression between brackets and composes through chained
index, named-property, call, and construction suffixes. The separate
`array-assignment-expressions` composition accepts local, captured, and
receiver bases ending in an index, including mixed forms such as
`holder.items[index] = value`. Indices in this initial array-specific layer must
be nonnegative i30 values; general computed property keys require a later
`ToPropertyKey` composition.

Array and object literals keep a compact numeric-value parser when
`literal-expressions` is omitted. Enabling that compiler-only composition lets
each value use the ordinary supported expression grammar, including enclosing
bindings, nested literals, and guest calls. It adds no opcode or runtime crate:
the resulting values still use `set_index` and `set_property`. Computed keys,
spread properties/elements, elisions, and shorthand properties remain separate
syntax work.

The removable integer-operations slice adds subtraction, multiplication, and
`<`/`>` comparisons with ordinary multiplicative, additive, then relational
precedence. Together with local assignment and `while`, it can run a
source-compiled factorial loop with `n > 1` as its stopping condition without
linking boxed IEEE-754 numbers. Results outside the i30 range currently fail
rather than promote, so this is a deliberately bounded programming subset, not
yet full JavaScript numeric semantics.

JavaScript Number division and remainder are independent product-level
features. `/` produces IEEE-754 semantics, including infinities and NaN. `%`
preserves immediate integer results where possible and boxes cases such as
negative zero or non-integer operands. They have separate modules, bytecodes,
compiler gates, and VM match arms because the linked floating remainder helper
is much larger than division. Omitting either token family leaves it unconsumed
as ordinary unexpected syntax.

Unary numeric negation is independently removable from those binary
operations. Its bounded build accepts i30 operands and reports overflow; the
JavaScript Number composition keeps nonzero i30 results immediate, promotes
the asymmetric minimum, boxes `-0` so its sign remains observable, and applies
primitive Number coercion. Unary `+` is its own removable bytecode/compiler
gate: booleans and null become immediate i30 values, undefined becomes boxed
NaN, and existing i30 and boxed numbers are retained. Strings and objects are
rejected until separately removable string parsing and `ToPrimitive`
compositions exist. Prefix operators are collected iteratively before
emission, so mixed chains do not recurse on the native stack. Compiler builds
with only one unary family retain a smaller specialized scanner. A compiler
selecting only `number-negation` can emit the promoted instruction without
also retaining decimal and exponent literal parsing; selecting `numbers` adds
those literal forms.

The independent `string-number-coercion` composition extends those same unary
instructions to string operands. It trims the ECMAScript whitespace and line
terminator set, accepts decimal `StringNumericLiteral`, signed `Infinity`, and
unsigned binary/octal/hex integer forms, preserves negative zero, and returns
NaN for invalid input. It does not add object conversion or make every
string-enabled runtime retain numeric parsing. Decimal source literals and
this coercion share `microesnext-decimal-to-f64`; omitting both removes its
exact fast path and bounded correctly-rounded fallback from the linked build.

Strict equality and inequality (`===` and `!==`) are a separate removable
capability. Immediate values, objects, and symbols compare by identity. When
composed with strings, independently allocated equal strings compare by
content; when composed with numbers, `+0` equals `-0` and `NaN` remains unequal
to itself. This is deliberately separate from the SameValueZero operation used
by Map and Set, where `NaN` keys compare equal. Coercive `==` and `!=` are not
yet included.

These scalar features are independent. In particular, `equality,numbers`
contains no string constructor, string bytecode, atom table, or string-aware
equality path. Guest code in that build has no operation that can create a
string value or place one in a variable; adding numeric equality does not imply
a general dynamically typed scalar bundle.

`typeof` is independently removable and returns the standard strings for
undefined, booleans, numbers, strings, symbols, functions, and objects,
including JavaScript's `typeof null === "object"` rule. Boxed numbers, symbols,
async closures, and Promise resolver functions participate only when their
separate representation features are composed. Repeated and mixed unary forms
such as `typeof typeof value` and `typeof +value` preserve right-to-left unary
evaluation. A build without `typeof` does not consume the keyword.

Ordinary `instanceof` is split across callable-object records, property
lookup, and allocation-free prototype traversal. The operator has its own
bytecode, compiler, VM, and facade gates; omitting it leaves the keyword
unconsumed and preserves the pre-existing relational parser. Its current
runtime accepts callable objects with an ordinary object-valued `prototype`
property. With the independent `constructors` gate, synchronous `function`
declarations and expressions are wrapped in ordinary callable objects with a
cyclic `.prototype.constructor`; arrows and async functions remain compact,
non-constructible closure records. The separately removable `construction`
gate compiles direct `new Constructor(arguments)` expressions, creates a
receiver whose prototype is the constructor's current object-valued
`prototype`, exposes it as `this`, and implements the ordinary primitive- versus
object-return rule. The preserved receiver stays below the ordinary call frame,
so construction adds no persistent VM field. A direct identifier or member
constructor may omit parentheses and then uses the same zero-argument opcode.
Parenthesized constructor expressions, `Symbol.hasInstance`, bound functions,
and host constructors are not implied by this initial path. The constructed
result does support ordinary property and method suffixes, and a direct member
constructor such as `new namespace.Type()` resolves without retaining the
namespace as a method receiver. A named function's
private self cell is rebound to the wrapper by the independent
`recursive-constructors` composition, so calls and construction observe the
same function identity. Function declarations already reuse their ordinary
captured declaration cell and do not need that opcode.

The `classes` slice accepts a named declaration containing a constructor. It
emits a distinct class-constructor record rather than
lowering the declaration to an ordinary function, so calling it without `new`
is a type error. Constructor parameters, `this` property initialization,
explicit returns, and the implicit `this` result compose with existing receiver
and construction rules. The separate `class-methods` gate adds synchronous
instance methods with ordered parameters and receiver-preserving member calls.
Those methods are callable objects without constructor prototypes, so extracting
one loses its receiver and attempting to construct one fails. `extends`, fields,
accessors, generators, async methods, computed names, and static initialization
blocks are not included by these focused gates. The
compiler-only `class-static-methods` feature does support synchronous named
static methods. They live on the class record and receive that record as `this`.

The `class-expressions` composition accepts anonymous classes in expression
position. Explicit and default constructors, instance methods, and static
methods behave as they do in declarations, while the expression evaluates to
the decorated class record. Named class expressions and their private inner
binding are not yet included.

The compiler-only `class-default-constructors` feature also accepts empty and
method-only base classes. It synthesizes a zero-argument receiver frame that
returns `this`, then emits the same class-record operation as explicit source.
There is deliberately no runtime feature or special default-constructor opcode.
Derived default constructors await the separate `extends` composition because
they must forward arguments through `super` rather than merely return `this`.

Logical AND and OR (`&&` and `||`) are another removable composition. They
short-circuit evaluation and return the selected operand rather than coercing
it to a boolean. Unary `!` uses the same selected truthiness policy and returns
a tagged boolean. The compiler counts repeated negations before parsing their
operand, avoiding native recursion for inputs such as `!!!!!!!!value`. Their
branch instructions retain the left operand when it
wins and pop it before evaluating the right operand otherwise. `&&` binds more
tightly than `||`, while strict equality and relational arithmetic bind above
both. Number-only and string-only truthiness are tested as separate Cargo
feature combinations.

Arrow functions support expression and block bodies. Lexical `this` and
`arguments`, and rejection as constructors, remain compiler work. Untagged
template literals support cooked text, escapes, multiline text, and
substitutions. Tagged templates are not included.

The Rust compiler currently emits single-quoted, double-quoted, and
no-substitution template strings directly into the removable string bytecode.
It accepts the compact escape set used by the current examples and preserves
UTF-8 source bytes. `${...}` substitution remains in the C behavioral oracle
until general JavaScript addition/coercion is composed in Rust; a string-only
compiler rejects it at the dollar sign rather than silently changing its
meaning.

Strict concatenation is a separate runtime and compiler capability. When both
operands are proven string literals, it emits `ConcatenateStrings` and joins
their arena bytes without a temporary Rust allocation. It deliberately does
not stand in for dynamic JavaScript `+`, `ToPrimitive`, or `ToString`.

The current compiler accepts `async function` declarations, anonymous async
function expressions, and async arrows with expression or block bodies.
Runtime async closures return their outer Promise before an awaited body
finishes, and nested async calls restore each ordinary caller in order—even
when the nested call begins after its caller has resumed. `await` on a value or
engine Promise resumes through the Promise job queue; synchronous throws
reject the outer Promise. Rejections after suspension propagate through nested
awaits and enter restored guest `catch` handlers. Named async expressions
retain a private self-binding through the removable recursive-async
composition. An optional async resolution policy assimilates object thenables
returned by an async function or used as the operand of `await` through the
bounded thenable-job queue. Source-visible Promise APIs, async methods, and
async generators remain compiler/runtime work rather than implied support.

The current Rust compiler slice also emits function declarations, anonymous
function expressions, single- or parenthesized-parameter arrows, postfix calls,
and ordered parameter slots directly to bytecode. Arrow expression and block
bodies share the ordinary closure compiler rather than adding an opcode. A
function expression may be called immediately or retained in a local, and all
three forms use the same capture analysis. Both declared parameters and call
arguments have a `u16` count. Argument-to-local frame setup is a removable
capability rather than a cost borne by functions without parameters. Named
function expressions get a private self-binding backed by a removable
recursive-function constructor, allowing recursion without leaking the name
into the enclosing scope. The optional receiver runtime can enter a closure
with an explicit receiver, expose it through receiver-specific frame bytecode,
and leave the frame without changing ordinary parameter/local positions.
Source compilation of `arguments` and full declaration hoisting remains
compiler work. Receiver-preserving member calls are an independent composition:
ordinary property reads still discard the base, while a property immediately
followed by a call retains the base only until the method frame is entered.

Mutable closure semantics use optional traced environment cells. Compiler
capture analysis boxes only bindings that cross a function boundary; sibling
closures and later writes by the outer frame share the same cell. This avoids
turning every lexical local into a heap allocation merely because some builds
support closures. A self-referential declaration initializes its closure into
that same cell, providing the identity needed by recursive calls. Nested
function declarations are appended with explicit bytecode relocation records;
captures can be forwarded through intermediate functions without allocating a
second cell for the same binding. Branch, loop, catch, and nested-function
targets share that relocation path. Recursive source calls therefore stay on
guest VM frames rather than consuming the native Rust stack.

The Rust vertical slice directly emits bytecode for a deliberately narrow
`try { return|throw expression } catch [(name)] { return|throw expression }`
form, with fallthrough joins behind `nonterminal-exceptions` and nested regions
behind `nested-exceptions`. Catch targets are backpatched without an AST.
Handler records and caught values live on the guest stack, survive `await`, and
are removed on normal and abrupt paths. Nested lexical environments, `finally`,
and the complete ECMAScript grammar remain future compiler work.

## Collections

Unique symbols are available as a removable primitive and compose directly as
object, Map, and Set keys. Descriptions may be absent or ordinary strings, but
do not affect equality. The global symbol registry remains future work. The
realm-owned `Symbol.iterator` identity is a separate capability rather than
making every symbol build retain realm state or an atom table. Small realms can
omit its description string, while string-capable realms can supply the
standard `"Symbol.iterator"` description.

`Map` and `Set` provide constructors from arrays, ordered storage,
SameValueZero key comparison, `size`, `get`, `set`, `add`, `has`, `delete`,
`clear`, `forEach`, and stateful `keys`, `values`, and `entries` iterators.
Iterators remain live while entries are deleted or appended.
SameValueZero is statically composed: boxed NaNs and signed zero work when the
number capability is present, and independently allocated equal strings work
when strings are present. Neither comparison path enters collection-only
builds.

The Rust engine keeps iterator cursors in the guest arena, so they relocate and
retain their source without a native allocation. Array-value iteration and
Map/Set key/value iteration are independent adapters. Map entry iteration is a
third adapter because its pair results require array allocation. A separate
result-object capability creates relocatable `{ value, done }` objects using
cached atom identities. It accepts the same allocation-free `Option<Value>`
returned by either source adapter. Cursor-only builds omit its object, atom, and string
dependencies.
Entry-pair allocation and guest calls remain above this layer. Binding and
inherited lookup of the realm-local `Symbol.iterator` are available separately
from function validation and invocation; this keeps the protocol identity
useful without forcing closures into every iterable build.

`WeakMap` and `WeakSet` provide constructors from arrays and their usual
`get`, `set`, `add`, `has`, and `delete` operations. Keys must be objects. The
collector treats their entries as ephemerons: a live key retains its value,
while a collection alone does not retain its keys.

## Promise

The current runtime slice provides compact pending/fulfilled/rejected records,
reaction lists, and a caller-owned FIFO job ring. Optional native resolution
adopts another engine Promise, including its eventual rejection, without
linking object property lookup. Async function return uses this path.

Self-resolution rejects with a compact guest TypeError value. Optional
one-shot resolving functions share an `alreadyResolved` cell. The thenable
composition reads an ordinary object's data-property `then`, verifies that it
is a guest closure, and schedules it with the object as `this`. A throw before
resolution rejects; a throw or second resolver call afterward is ignored.
Resolving with another thenable returns a separately rooted follow-up job, so
chains remain deferred and never recurse on the Rust or WebAssembly stack.

Accessors and proxies for `then`, async `then` closures, source-visible
`Promise`, `then`, `catch`, `finally`, `resolve`, `reject`, `all`, and `race`,
subclass constructors, species, and unhandled-rejection reporting remain work
rather than claimed support.

## Editor-driven scope

The surface was checked against generated CodeMirror, ProseMirror, and
WordGard bundles in `dev/wasm-web-runtimes/examples/browser-editors`. Notable
requirements include WordGard's stateful `map.keys().next()` listener queue,
ProseMirror's deferred `Promise.resolve().then(...)` observer flush, and the
three editors' ordered maps, sets, weak caches, and `Promise.all` joins.

`test/editor-primitives.test.js` keeps those observed patterns executable. It
tests the primitives independently of each editor's DOM facade and of syntax
features that MicroESNext has not added yet.

Browser editors follow the same boundary as the QuickJS and MicroQuickJS
examples. Guest JavaScript owns the browser-shaped DOM facade, event behavior,
and application-specific overrides. The native runtime supplies opaque host
references, their lifetime notifications, message transport, and small
explicit primitives such as time. A future integration may forward an
individual well-audited operation natively when measurement justifies it, but
native forwarding is opt-in per operation rather than an all-or-nothing host
object model. The compiler's caller-provided host-name list is a source probe;
it is not the browser integration API.


<!-- dev/microquickjs-experiment/src/README.md -->

MicroQuickJS
============

## Introduction

MicroQuickJS (aka. MQuickJS) is a JavaScript engine targeted at
embedded systems. It compiles and runs JavaScript programs using as little
as 10 kB of RAM. The whole engine requires about 100 kB of ROM (ARM
Thumb-2 code) including the C library. The speed is comparable to
QuickJS.

MQuickJS only supports a [subset](#javascript-subset-reference) of JavaScript close to ES5. It
implements a **stricter mode** where some error prone or inefficient
JavaScript constructs are forbidden.

Although MQuickJS shares much code with QuickJS, it internals are
different in order to consume less memory. In particular, it relies on
a tracing garbage collector, the VM does not use the CPU stack and
strings are stored in UTF-8.

## REPL

The REPL is `mqjs`. Usage:

```
usage: mqjs [options] [file [args]]
-h  --help            list options
-e  --eval EXPR       evaluate EXPR
-i  --interactive     go to interactive mode
-I  --include file    include an additional file
-d  --dump            dump the memory usage stats
    --memory-limit n  limit the memory usage to 'n' bytes
--no-column           no column number in debug information
-o FILE               save the bytecode to FILE
-m32                  force 32 bit bytecode output (use with -o)
-b  --allow-bytecode  allow bytecode in input file
```

Compile and run a program using 10 kB of RAM:

```sh
./mqjs --memory-limit 10k tests/mandelbrot.js
```


In addition to normal script execution, `mqjs` can output the compiled
bytecode to a persistent storage (file or ROM):

```sh
./mqjs -o mandelbrot.bin tests/mandelbrot.js
```

Then you can run the compiled bytecode as a normal script:

```sh
./mqjs -b mandelbrot.bin
```

The bytecode format depends on the endianness and word length (32 or
64 bit) of the CPU. On a 64 bit CPU, it is possible to use the option
`-m32` to generate 32 bit bytecode that can run on an embedded 32 bit
system.

Use the option `--no-column` to remove the column number debug info
(only line numbers are remaining) if you want to save some storage.

## Stricter mode

MQuickJS only supports a subset of JavaScript (mostly ES5). It is
always in **stricter** mode where some error prone JavaScript features
are disabled. The general idea is that the stricter mode is a subset
of JavaScript, so it still works as usual in other JavaScript
engines. Here are the main points:

- Only **strict mode** constructs are allowed, hence no `with` keyword
  and global variables must be declared with the `var` keyword.

- Arrays cannot have holes. Writing an element after the end is not
  allowed:
```js
    a = []
    a[0] = 1; // OK to extend the array length
    a[10] = 2; // TypeError
```
  If you need an array like object with holes, use a normal object
  instead:
```js
    a = {}
    a[0] = 1;
    a[10] = 2;
```
  `new Array(len)` still works as expected, but the array elements are
  initialized to `undefined`.
  Array literals with holes are a syntax error:
```js
    [ 1, , 3 ] // SyntaxError
```
- Only global `eval` is supported so it cannot access to nor modify
  local variables:
```js
    eval('1 + 2'); // forbidden
    (1, eval)('1 + 2'); // OK
```
- No value boxing: `new Number(1)` is not supported and never
  necessary.

## JavaScript Subset Reference
 
- Only strict mode is supported with emphasis on ES5 compatibility.

- `Array` objects:

    - They have no holes.
    
    - Numeric properties are always handled by the array object and not
      forwarded to its prototype.
  
    - Out-of-bound sets are an error except when they are at the end of
      the array.
      
    - The `length` property is a getter/setter in the array prototype.

- all properties are writable, enumerable and configurable.

- `for in` only iterates over the object own properties. It should be
  used with this common pattern to have a consistent behavior with
  standard JavaScript:
  
```js
    for(var prop in obj) {
        if (obj.hasOwnProperty(prop)) {
            ...
        }
    }
```    
Always prefer using `for of` instead which is supported with arrays:

```js
    for(var prop of Object.keys(obj)) {
        ...
    }
```

- `prototype`, `length` and `name` are getter/setter in function objects.

- C functions cannot have their own properties (but C constructors
  behave as expected).

- The global object is supported, but its use is discouraged. It
  cannot contain getter/setters and properties directly created in it
  are not visible as global variables in the executing script.

- The variable associated with the `catch` keyword is a normal
  variable.

- Direct `eval` is not supported. Only indirect (=global) `eval` is
  supported.

- No value boxing (e.g. `new Number(1)` is not supported)

- Regexp:

    - case folding only works with ASCII characters.

    - the matching is unicode only i.e. `/./` matches a unicode code
      point instead of an UTF-16 character as with the `u` flag.

- String: `toLowerCase` / `toUpperCase` only handle ASCII characters.

- Date: only `Date.now()` is supported.

ES5 extensions:
  
- `for of` is supported but iterates only over arrays. No custom
   iterator is supported (yet).

- Typed arrays.

- `\u{hex}` is accepted in string literals

- Math functions: `imul`, `clz32`, `fround`, `trunc`, `log2`, `log10`.

- The exponentiation operator

- Regexp: the dotall (`s`), sticky (`y`) and unicode (`u`) flags are
  accepted. In unicode mode, the unicode properties are not supported.

- String functions: `codePointAt`, `replaceAll`, `trimStart`, `trimEnd`.

- The `globalThis` global property.

## C API

### Engine initialization

MQuickJS has almost no dependency on the C library. In particular it
does not use `malloc()`, `free()` nor `printf()`. When creating a
MQuickJS context, a memory buffer must be provided. The engine only
allocates memory in this buffer:
```c
    JSContext *ctx;
    uint8_t mem_buf[8192];
    ctx = JS_NewContext(mem_buf, sizeof(mem_buf), &js_stdlib);
    ...
    JS_FreeContext(ctx);
```
`JS_FreeContext(ctx)` is only necessary to call the finalizers of user
objects as no system memory is allocated by the engine.

### Memory handling

The C API is very similar to QuickJS (see `mquickjs.h`). However,
since there is a compacting garbage collector, there are important
differences:

1. Explicitly freeing values is not necessary (no `JS_FreeValue()`).

2. The address of objects can move each time a JS allocation is
called. The general rule is to avoid having variables of type
`JSValue` in C. They may be present only for temporary use between
MQuickJS API calls. In the other cases, always use a pointer to a
`JSValue`. `JS_PushGCRef()` returns a pointer to a temporary opaque
`JSValue` stored in a `JSGCRef` variable. `JS_PopGCRef()` must be used
to release the temporary reference. The opaque value in `JSGCRef` is
automatically updated when objects move. Example:

```c
JSValue my_js_func(JSContext *ctx, JSValue *this_val, int argc, JSValue *argv)
{
        JSGCRef obj1_ref, obj2_ref;
        JSValue *obj1, *obj2, ret;

        ret = JS_EXCEPTION;
        obj1 = JS_PushGCRef(ctx, &obj1_ref);
        obj2 = JS_PushGCRef(ctx, &obj2_ref);
        *obj1 = JS_NewObject(ctx);
        if (JS_IsException(*obj1))
            goto fail;
        *obj2 = JS_NewObject(ctx); // obj1 may move
        if (JS_IsException(*obj2))
            goto fail;
        JS_SetPropertyStr(ctx, *obj1, "x", *obj2);  // obj1 and obj2 may move
        ret = *obj1;
     fail:
        PopGCRef(ctx, &obj2_ref);
        PopGCRef(ctx, &obj1_ref);
        return ret;
}
```

When running on a PC, the `DEBUG_GC` define can be used to force the
JS allocator to always move objects at each allocation. It is a good
way to check no invalid JSValue is used.

### Standard library

The standard library is compiled by a custom tool (`mquickjs_build.c`)
to C structures that may reside in ROM. Hence the standard library
instantiation is very fast and requires almost no RAM. An example of
standard library for `mqjs` is provided in `mqjs_stdlib.c`. The result
of its compilation is `mqjs_stdlib.h`.

`example.c` is a complete example using the MQuickJS C API.

### Persistent bytecode

The bytecode generated by `mqjs` may be executed from ROM. In this
case, it must be relocated before being flashed into ROM (see
`JS_RelocateBytecode()`). It is then instantiated with
`JS_LoadBytecode()` and run as normal script with `JS_Run()` (see
`mqjs.c`).

As with QuickJS, no backward compatibility is guaranteed at the
bytecode level. Moreover, the bytecode is not verified before being
executed. Only run JavaScript bytecode from trusted sources.

### Mathematical library and floating point emulation

MQuickJS contains its own tiny mathematical library (in
`libm.c`). Moreover, in case the CPU has no floating point support, it
contains its own floating point emulator which may be smaller than the
one provided with the GCC toolchain.

## Internals and comparison with QuickJS

### Garbage collection

A tracing and compacting garbage collector is used instead of
reference counting. It allows smaller objects. The GC adds an overhead
of a few bits per allocated memory block. Moreover, memory
fragmentation is avoided.

The engine has its own memory allocator and does not depend on the C
library malloc.

### Value and object representation

The value has the same size as a CPU word (hence 32 bits on a 32 bit
CPU). A value may contain:

  - a 31 bit integer (1 bit tag)

  - a single unicode codepoint (hence a string of one or two 16 bit code units)

  - a 64 bit floating point number with a small exponent with 64 bit CPU words

  - a pointer to a memory block. Memory blocks have a tag stored in
    memory.

JavaScript objects require at least 3 CPU words (hence 12 bytes on a
32 bit CPU). Additional data may be allocated depending on the object
class. The properties are stored in a hash table. Each property
requires at least 3 CPU words. Properties may reside in ROM for
standard library objects.

Property keys are JSValues unlike QuickJS where they have a specific
type. They are either a string or a positive 31 bit integer. String
property keys are internalized (unique).

Strings are internally stored in WTF-8 (UTF-8 + unpaired surrogates)
instead of 8 or 16 bit arrays in QuickJS. Surrogate pairs are not
stored explicitly but are still visible when iterating thru 16 bit
code units in JavaScript. Hence full compatibility with JavaScript and
UTF-8 is maintained.

C Functions can be stored as a single value to reduce the overhead. In
this case, no additional properties can be added. Most standard
library functions are stored this way.

### Standard library

The whole standard library resides in ROM. It is generated at compile
time. Only a few objects are created in RAM. Hence the engine
instantiation time is very low.

### Bytecode

It is a stack based bytecode (similar to QuickJS). However, the
bytecode references atoms thru an indirect table.

Line and column number information is compressed with 
[exponential-Golomb codes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exponential-Golomb_coding).

### Compilation

The parser is very close to the QuickJS one but it avoids recursion so
the C stack usage is bounded. There is no abstract syntax tree. The
bytecode is generated in one pass with several tricks to optimize it
(QuickJS has several optimization passes).

## Tests and benchmarks

Running the basic tests:
```sh
make test
```

Running the QuickJS micro benchmark:
```sh
make microbench
```

Additional tests and a patched version of the Octane benchmark running
in stricter mode can be downloaded
[here](https://bellard.org/mquickjs/mquickjs-extras.tar.xz):

Running the V8 octane benchmark:
```sh
make octane
```
